It is affectionately known as “the walrus operator” due to\nits resemblance to the eyes and tusks of a walrus .\nIn this example, the assignment expression helps avoid calling\nlen() twice:\nif ( n := len ( a )) > 10 :\nprint ( f \"List is too long ( { n } elements, expected <= 10)\" )\nA similar benefit arises during regular expression matching where\nmatch objects are needed twice, once to test whether a match\noccurred and another to extract a subgroup:\ndiscount = 0.0\nif ( mo := re . search ( r '(\\d+) % d iscount' , advertisement )):\ndiscount = float ( mo . group ( 1 )) / 100.0\nThe operator is also useful with while-loops that compute\na value to test loop termination and then need that same\nvalue again in the body of the loop:\n# Loop over fixed length blocks\nwhile ( block := f . read ( 256 )) != '' :\nprocess ( block )\nAnother motivating use case arises in list comprehensions where\na value computed in a filtering condition is also needed in\nthe expression body:\n[ clean_name . title () for name in names\nif ( clean_name := normalize ( 'NFC' , name )) in allowed_names ]\nTry to limit use of the walrus operator to clean cases that reduce\ncomplexity and improve readability.\nSee PEP 572 for a full description.\n(Contributed by Emily Morehouse in bpo-35224 .)\nPositional-only parameters ¶\nThere is a new function parameter syntax / to indicate that some\nfunction parameters must be specified positionally and cannot be used as\nkeyword arguments. This is the same notation shown by help() for C\nfunctions annotated with Larry Hastings’\nArgument Clinic tool.\nIn the following example, parameters a and b are positional-only,\nwhile c or d can be positional or keyword, and e or f are\nrequired to be keywords:\ndef f ( a , b , / , c , d , * , e , f ):\nprint ( a , b , c , d , e , f )\nThe following is a valid call:\nf ( 10 , 20 , 30 , d = 40 , e = 50 , f = 60 )\nHowever, these are invalid calls:\nf ( 10 , b = 20 , c = 30 , d = 40 , e = 50 , f = 60 ) # b cannot be a keyword argument\n"}],"details":{}}}

It is affectionately known as “the walrus operator” due to\nits resemblance to the eyes and tusks of a walrus .\nIn this example, the assignment expression helps avoid calling\nlen() twice:\nif ( n := len ( a )) > 10 :\nprint ( f \"List is too long ( { n } elements, expected <= 10)\" )\nA similar benefit arises during regular expression matching where\nmatch objects are needed twice, once to test whether a match\noccurred and another to extract a subgroup:\ndiscount = 0.0\nif ( mo := re . search ( r '(\\d+) % d iscount' , advertisement )):\ndiscount = float ( mo . group ( 1 )) / 100.0\nThe operator is also useful with while-loops that compute\na value to test loop termination and then need that same\nvalue again in the body of the loop:\n# Loop over fixed length blocks\nwhile ( block := f . read ( 256 )) != '' :\nprocess ( block )\nAnother motivating use case arises in list comprehensions where\na value computed in a filtering condition is also needed in\nthe expression body:\n[ clean_name . title () for name in names\nif ( clean_name := normalize ( 'NFC' , name )) in allowed_names ]\nTry to limit use of the walrus operator to clean cases that reduce\ncomplexity and improve readability.\nSee PEP 572 for a full description.\n(Contributed by Emily Morehouse in bpo-35224 .)\nPositional-only parameters ¶\nThere is a new function parameter syntax / to indicate that some\nfunction parameters must be specified positionally and cannot be used as\nkeyword arguments. This is the same notation shown by help() for C\nfunctions annotated with Larry Hastings’\nArgument Clinic tool.\nIn the following example, parameters a and b are positional-only,\nwhile c or d can be positional or keyword, and e or f are\nrequired to be keywords:\ndef f ( a , b , / , c , d , * , e , f ):\nprint ( a , b , c , d , e , f )\nThe following is a valid call:\nf ( 10 , 20 , 30 , d = 40 , e = 50 , f = 60 )\nHowever, these are invalid calls:\nf ( 10 , b = 20 , c = 30 , d = 40 , e = 50 , f = 60 ) # b cannot be a keyword argument\n"}]},"isError":false}

This is the same notation shown by help() for C\nfunctions annotated with Larry Hastings’\nArgument Clinic tool.\nIn the following example, parameters a and b are positional-only,\nwhile c or d can be positional or keyword, and e or f are\nrequired to be keywords:\ndef f ( a , b , / , c , d , * , e , f ):\nprint ( a , b , c , d , e , f )\nThe following is a valid call:\nf ( 10 , 20 , 30 , d = 40 , e = 50 , f = 60 )\nHowever, these are invalid calls:\nf ( 10 , b = 20 , c = 30 , d = 40 , e = 50 , f = 60 ) # b cannot be a keyword argument\n"}],"isError":false,"timestamp":1786864217036}}

User passes `proxies = {'https': 'http://user:pass@proxy.example.com:8080'}` (auth embedded in proxy URL).\n2. In `send()` → `get_connection(request.url, proxies)` → `select_proxy(url, proxies)` returns the proxy URL string with auth.\n3. Then `proxy = prepend_scheme_if_needed(proxy, 'http')`. Since the proxy URL already has a scheme (`http`), `parse_url` parses it. Note: `parse_url` (urllib3's) separates auth: `scheme='http'`, `auth='user:pass'`, `host='proxy.example.com'`, `port=8080`. **But `parsed.netloc` does NOT include the auth portion** — that's the urllib3 behavior: `netloc` is `host:port` only.\n4. So the returned URL becomes `http://proxy.example.com:8080` — **the auth portion is dropped!**\n5. Then `proxy_manager_for(proxy)` is called with this auth-less URL. Inside, `proxy_headers(proxy)` calls `get_auth_from_url(proxy)` which uses `urlparse` and extracts username/password — but there's no username/password anymore since auth was dropped. So `Proxy-Authorization` header is never set.\n6. The proxy receives a request without credentials → 407.\n\nWhy does it only affect Python 3.8.12? Because `prepend_scheme_if_needed` uses urllib3's `parse_url`, which is version-dependent. Wait, the user is on urllib3 1.26.7. Hmm, actually the change that caused this was in requests itself — the switch from `urlparse` to `parse_url` (urllib3) in `prepend_scheme_if_needed`, which happened in commit `b0829a86 Merge pull request #6020 from nateprewitt/proxy_scheme_unknown_fix` — \"Update 3.10-dev to 3.10 and add pypy-3.7\"... Actually the PR #6020 is \"proxy_scheme_unknown_fix\".\n\nWait, but the user says Python 3.8.12 vs other Python versions. That's odd — the parse_url behavior would be the same across Python versions. But actually it doesn't matter for the fix; the issue reporter just noticed it on 3.8.12 (maybe different urllib3 version resolution or something). The fix is what matters: restore the auth portion when prepending the scheme.\n\nSo the fix (PR #6028) modifies `prepend_scheme_if_needed` in `requests/utils.py`:\n\n```python\n    parsed = parse_url(url)\n    scheme, auth, host, port, path, query, fragment = parsed\n\n    # A defect in urlparse determines that there isn't a netloc present in some\n    # urls. In `send()` → `get_connection(request.url, proxies)` → `select_proxy(url, proxies)` returns the proxy URL string with auth.\n3. Then `proxy = prepend_scheme_if_needed(proxy, 'http')`. Since the proxy URL already has a scheme (`http`), `parse_url` parses it. Note: `parse_url` (urllib3's) separates auth: `scheme='http'`, `auth='user:pass'`, `host='proxy.example.com'`, `port=8080`. **But `parsed.netloc` does NOT include the auth portion** — that's the urllib3 behavior: `netloc` is `host:port` only.\n4. So the returned URL becomes `http://proxy.example.com:8080` — **the auth portion is dropped!**\n5. Then `proxy_manager_for(proxy)` is called with this auth-less URL. Inside, `proxy_headers(proxy)` calls `get_auth_from_url(proxy)` which uses `urlparse` and extracts username/password — but there's no username/password anymore since auth was dropped. So `Proxy-Authorization` header is never set.\n6. The proxy receives a request without credentials → 407.\n\nWhy does it only affect Python 3.8.12? In `send()` → `get_connection(request.url, proxies)` → `select_proxy(url, proxies)` returns the proxy URL string with auth.\n3. Then `proxy = prepend_scheme_if_needed(proxy, 'http')`. Since the proxy URL already has a scheme (`http`), `parse_url` parses it. Note: `parse_url` (urllib3's) separates auth: `scheme='http'`, `auth='user:pass'`, `host='proxy.example.com'`, `port=8080`. **But `parsed.netloc` does NOT include the auth portion** — that's the urllib3 behavior: `netloc` is `host:port` only.\n4. That helper uses urllib3's `parse_url`, which splits the URL into `scheme`, `auth`, `host`, `port`, etc., and its `netloc` property **excludes the auth portion**. The old implementation rebuilt the URL from `netloc` alone, silently dropping `user:pass@` from proxy URLs.\n\nWith the credentials stripped, `proxy_headers()` (which calls `get_auth_from_url()`) found no username, so the `Proxy-Authorization` header was never sent to the proxy — the proxy responded with **407**.\n\n## Fix\n\nIn `requests/utils.py`, `prepend_scheme_if_needed()` now re-attaches the parsed `auth` component to the `netloc` before rebuilding the URL:\n\n```python\nif auth:\n    # parse_url doesn't provide the netloc with auth\n    # so we'll add it ourselves.\n    netloc = '@'.join([auth, netloc])\n```\n\nPlus regression tests in `tests/test_utils.py` covering `user:pass@` and `user@` proxy URLs.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Before: `prepend_scheme_if_needed('http://user:pass@example.com:8080', 'http')` → `'http://example.com:8080'` (auth dropped)\n- After: → `'http://user:pass@example.com:8080'`, and `proxy_headers()` correctly emits `Proxy-Authorization: Basic dXNlcjpwYXNz`\n- `tests/test_utils.py`: 203 passed, 11 skipped (pre-existing env issues only, identical to baseline)"}],"api":"openai-completions","provider":"opencode-go","model":"deepseek-v4-flash","usage":{"input":418,"output":389,"cacheRead":22016,"cacheWrite":0,"reasoning":0,"totalTokens":22823,"cost":{"input":0.00002926,"output":0.000054460000000000004,"cacheRead":0.0000308224,"cacheWrite":0,"total":0.0001145424}},"stopReason":"stop","timestamp":1786864393567,"responseId":"4e138953-9e9a-4791-93fd-c9a18015ce16","rawStopReason":"stop"}}
